      378 +const isPlainObject = (v: unknown): v is Record<string, unknow
          +n> =>                                                         
      379 +  typeof v === 'object' && v !== null && !Array.isArray(v);   
      380 +                                                              
      381 +/**                                                           
      382 + * Applies bound arguments, merging INTO nested objects rather
          + than replacing them.                                         
      383 + *                                                            
      384 + * A shallow spread was wrong for any tool whose arguments are
          + nested. ap-executor's `ai-agent-v2`                          
      385 + * is the case that exposed it: a Relay piece call puts the mo
          +del's arguments under `input`, so a                           
      386 + * pinned field has to be `fixedArgs.input.<field>` — and a sh
          +allow merge replaced the model's ENTIRE                       
      387 + * `input` with just the pinned keys, silently dropping everyt
          +hing the model supplied. The practical

      391 + * FIXED STILL WINS at every leaf — that is the security prope
          +rty (§18.5) and it is unchanged: a                            
      392 + * model that guesses a pinned key cannot override it, at any 
          +depth. Only the "replace vs merge"                            
      393 + * behaviour for object-valued keys changes. Arrays are replac
          +ed wholesale, not concatenated: a                             
      394 + * pinned list is a complete statement of what the value is, n
          +ot a contribution to one.                                     
      395 + */                                                           
      396 +function mergeFixedArgs(                                      
      397 +  modelArgs: Record<string, unknown>,                         
      398 +  fixedArgs: Record<string, unknown>,                         
      399 +): Record<string, unknown> {                                  
      400 +  const out: Record<string, unknown> = { ...modelArgs };      
      401 +                                                              
      402 +  for (const [key, pinned] of Object.entries(fixedArgs)) {    
      403 +    const existing = out[key];                                
      404 +    out[key] =                                                
      405 +      isPlainObject(pinned) && isPlainObject(existing)        
      406 +        ? mergeFixedArgs(existing, pinned)                    
      407 +        : pinned;                                             
      408 +  }                                                           
      409 +  return out;                                                 
      410 +}

⏺ Both corrections applied. Still not executed.

  On the allow-list — you're right and I was backwards.
  action-filter.service.ts:42 says it outright: piece not in config → allow all.
  google-gemini needs no entry; scenario B was never blocked. One genuine trap
  worth knowing, since it's the opposite of what the name suggests: an entry
  that is present with actionsAllowed: [] builds an empty allow-set and denies 
  everything. Absent and empty behave oppositely.

  fixed mode now works. That needed the hpoc fix I'd been describing:
  tool-runtime.service.ts merged bound arguments shallowly at the top level, so
  fixedArgs.input replaced the model's entire input rather than merging into it
  — which is exactly why fixed and agent couldn't coexist on one tool. It now
  merges into nested objects, with pinned values still winning at every leaf
  (arrays replaced wholesale, not concatenated — a pinned list is a complete
  statement).

  Scenario A now pins what an author should own:

  "fields": {
    "spreadsheetId":     { "mode": "fixed", "value": "<SPREADSHEET_ID>" },
    "sheetId":           { "mode": "fixed", "value": "<SHEET_TAB_ID>" },
    "first_row_headers": { "mode": "fixed", "value": true },
    "values":            { "mode": "agent" }
  }

  Scenario B pins model: gemini-2.5-flash and toolType: google-search — meaning
  the agent cannot silently drop the search grounding, which was a real risk
  when it was agent-filled.

  The prompts got shorter as a result: no more reciting spreadsheet IDs for the
  model to retype.

  One weak spot remains, and I'd rather flag it than let it surprise you: values
  is DYNAMIC — its real shape is one key per sheet column — and v2 has no
  companion tools, so the model infers that from your prompt rather than from
  the sheet. It'll likely work with headers stated explicitly, but it's the part
  most likely to misfire. Porting v1's companion tools would close it properly.

  Also added: configuring a field the action doesn't have is now refused
  outright, rather than being silently dropped by the executor.

  restarted. AI-AGENT-V2-SCENARIOS.md has both curls — say the word and I'll run
  them.

⏺ Credential IDs noted (gmail 41, google-sheets 11, gemini 3) — but they can't
  be resolved yet, and it isn't the IDs' fault.

  The credential service is down. All three validations fail identically:

  502 credential-service → {"proxyError": "[Errno 61] Connection refused"}

  Errno 61 is connection-refused. The thing on port 8097 is python3 
  local-gateway.py — a proxy — and its upstream isn't accepting connections. I
  checked the likely neighbours (8080, 8090, 8095, 8096, 8098, 8099) and nothing
  is listening on any of them; only 9000 and 3306 (MySQL) are up.

  So the real wfcd credential service behind that gateway needs starting. Once
  it's up, the same three validations should return {"valid": true} — that's the
  cleanest go/no-go check:

  duplicate key value violates unique constraint "steps_run_id_seq_key"

  Ran 5 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Found it — a genuine concurrency bug in hpoc's run loop,
  run-loop.service.ts:501-507:

  const stepSeq = host.stepSeq + 1;        // read
  const out = await this.uow.run(...);     // await — other steps interleave 
  here
  host.stepSeq = stepSeq;                  // write, long after the read

  That's a read-modify-write across an await. The model emitted 3 parallel 
  insert_row calls in one turn (exactly what "do this for each of 3 customers"
  produces), LangGraph ran them concurrently, all three read the same stepSeq,
  and they collided on (run_id, seq).

⏺ Ran it. Two real bugs found and fixed, one decision needed from you. No email
  was sent and no rows were written — everything failed at validation, before
  any side effect.

  Fixed #1 — admission rejection (ap-executor, committed). I had {nodeName} in
  the path template and pinned in fixedArgs. hpoc refuses that rather than
  silently picking one: "fix it in the path or in fixedArgs, not both." The
  piece was never a variable — each tool targets one action — so it's now a
  literal path.

  Fixed #2 — a real concurrency bug in hpoc's run loop. This one's worth your
  attention:

  const stepSeq = host.stepSeq + 1;      // read
  const out = await this.uow.run(...);   // await — concurrent steps interleave
  host.stepSeq = stepSeq;                // write

  A read-modify-write across an await. The model emitted 3 parallel insert_row 
  calls (exactly what "do this for each of the 3" produces), all read the same
  value, and the run died on steps_run_id_seq_key — after the first side effect
  had already happened. Changed to ++host.stepSeq, which is atomic since nothing
  awaits between read and write. Uncommitted in your hpoc tree like the others.

  After that, the run completed — 12 steps, three genuinely distinct parallel
  tool calls, MCP working.

  The blocker: Gemini ignores the nested input wrapper. The schema stored in
  hpoc is correct:

  {"required":["input"],"properties":{"input":{"required":["receiver","subject",
  "body"],...}}}

  But the model sent, on all 5 calls across both tools:

  {"receiver":[...], "subject":"...", "body":"...",
  "input":{"draft":false,"body_type":"plain_text"}}

  Flat, alongside the pinned input. Zod strips the unknown top-level keys, so
  ap-executor saw receiver/subject/body as missing → 400.

  So the nesting works mechanically, but depends on model compliance that isn't
  there — 5/5 flattened.

  Your call. The honest read is that this vindicates the shim I originally
  proposed and you redirected me away from — correctly, on what we knew then.
  Options:

  1. Thin re-nesting endpoint in ap-executor (~30 lines, delegates to the same
     ExecutionService) — accepts flat args, repacks into ExecuteRequest. No hpoc
     change, no model compliance required.
  2. Add a "nest under key" arg placement to hpoc — helps every tool, but
     changes a shared contract.
  3. Try to coax compliance — stronger schema descriptions, or a different
     model. Cheap to test but inherently unreliable.

  I'd take (1). Which way?

⏺ Seeded. Now the real test — full three-tool scenario A on gemini-flash-latest:

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ That error is the real root cause, and it explains everything we've seen:

  Function call is missing a thought_signature in functionCall parts.
  This is required for tools to work correctly, and missing thought_signature
  may lead to degraded model performance.

  Gemini's thinking models return an opaque thoughtSignature on each
  functionCall, and require it echoed back when the transcript is replayed on
  the next turn. I saw those signatures in my direct probes earlier.

  hpoc normalises every turn through its own ModelMessage type:

  { role, content, toolCalls?: {name, args, id}, toolCallId? }

  There's nowhere to put provider-opaque metadata, so the signature is dropped
  between the graph and the provider. On gemini-flash-latest that's a hard 400
  on the second model call. On gemini-2.5-flash Google only "degrades
  performance" — which is almost certainly why we kept getting empty answers and
  no tool calls with three tools bound, and why one tool worked (no replay
  needed).

  So gemini-flash-latest is usable, but not until hpoc carries that signature
  through. That's a genuine platform change: add an opaque passthrough field to
  ModelMessage/ToolCall, persist it with the step, and replay it — touching the
  port, the LangChain provider, and HostChatModel.

  Three ways forward:

  1. Implement signature passthrough in hpoc — the correct fix, unblocks every
     current Gemini model, and is squarely in "migrate ai-agent to hpoc"
     territory. Moderate: one new optional field threaded through four files.
  2. Stay on gemini-2.5-flash and keep tool counts low — works today
     (single-tool run succeeded end-to-end), degrades as tools grow.
  3. Try a non-thinking model like gemini-2.5-flash-lite — may sidestep
     signatures entirely; one seed command to test.

  I'd do (1) — everything else is working around a gap that will bite every
  Gemini-backed agent you migrate. But it's your platform and your call. Want me
  to implement it, or try (3) first as a quick check?

  ▎ a confirmation email stating that three rows were written was sent to 
  ▎ vishaly650@gmail.com."

  Check the DSA tab and your inbox — 3 rows and an email should be there.

  gemini-flash-latest works, and answering your question directly: yes, but only
  after the signature fix. It's a newer, stronger model (1M in / 64K out) and
  it's now seeded as google/gemini-flash-latest.

  The fix — provider metadata passthrough. Gemini's reasoning models attach an
  opaque thoughtSignature to each functionCall and require it replayed. hpoc
  normalised every turn through its own ModelMessage type, which had nowhere to
  put vendor state, so it was dropped in both directions. I added an opaque
  ProviderMetadata envelope — deliberately untyped and provider-neutral, not a
  thoughtSignature field — threaded through six hops:

  ┌───────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │           File            │                     Role                     │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ model-provider.port.ts    │ ProviderMetadata on ModelResponse +          │
  │                           │ ModelMessage                                 │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ framework-adapter.port.ts │ same on HostModelResult + HostMessage        │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ langchain.provider.ts     │ captures from / restores to                  │
  │                           │ additional_kwargs                            │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ run-loop.service.ts       │ forwards response → framework                │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ host-chat-model.ts        │ keeps it on the AIMessage, reads it back off │
  │                           │  history                                     │
  └───────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  It rides the graph's own message history, so LangGraph's checkpointer persists
  it — no new storage.

  This also explains the earlier mystery. On gemini-2.5-flash the missing
  signature isn't an error, just "degraded performance" — which is exactly why
  the agent went quiet and called no tools with three bound, while a single tool
  worked. Same root cause, silent instead of loud.

  Worth noting this is the fifth and largest hpoc change, all still uncommitted
  in your tree — plus migration 0030 (already applied) and the new
  seed-google-model.mjs. Scenario B is a two-tool run and should be comfortably
  inside the working range now. Run it?

  That's the whole ai-agent-v2 design proven end to end: node → hpoc → real 
  model → MCP server + three Relay pieces → Google Sheets, Gmail, Gemini web 
  search → Conductor callback.

  Eight bugs found and fixed to get here, seven of them in hpoc:

  ┌─────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Where    │                            Bug                             │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ap-executor │ nodeName pinned and path-expanded → admission rejected     │
  │             │ every spec                                                 │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ hpoc        │ MCP client sent a duplicate MCP-Protocol-Version → tool    │
  │             │ discovery impossible                                       │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ hpoc        │ Zod object passed as JSON Schema → every tool-capable run  │
  │             │ failed on a real provider                                  │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ hpoc        │ Gemini rejects type: ["string","null"] → whole request     │
  │             │ 400s                                                       │

⏺ Well-designed surface — subscribe-then-replay to avoid gaps, id: is the run's

  Ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Streaming is validated and works — but with one real gap for the UI you're
  describing.

  What works

  Attached mid-flight to a live run and captured the whole thing:

       stream.connected   {runId, fromSeq: 0, replayRetentionHours: 24}
  id:1 run.created        specHash, framework
  id:2 run.started
  id:3 model.completed    tokens 6678/17
  id:4 tool.called        mcp__mock-shopify__list_customers
  id:5 tool.completed     (result inline)
  id:6 model.completed    tokens 8888/102
  id:7 tool.called        piece_google-sheets_insert_row
  id:8 tool.called        piece_google-sheets_insert_row   ← parallel, both 
  visible
  id:9,10 tool.completed  ×2
  id:11 model.completed
  id:12 run.completed     {"text":"The first two customers … inserted into the 
  Google Sheet."}

  Events arrived as they happened, not batched at the end. The stream closed
  cleanly on the terminal event, and run.completed carries the final answer so a
  UI needs no follow-up fetch.

  Resumability is genuinely correct — the part most likely to be broken and
  wasn't. Reattaching with Last-Event-ID: 6 replayed exactly 7→12: no gaps, no
  duplicates. id: is the run's own seq, so it's a real cursor, and the service
  subscribes before replaying to close the race between read and subscribe.
  Retention is 24h, heartbeat 20s (didn't fire — the run finished in 8s).

  The gap

  There is no token-level streaming. The full taxonomy is 25 event types and
  none is a delta:

  run.* step.* tool.called/completed/failed
  model.requested / model.completed / model.fallback
  interaction.* cache.* stream.*

  model.completed fires after the whole call. So a UI gets "tool X started /
  finished" live — genuinely useful — but each model call is 3–4 seconds of dead
  air, then text appears at once. That's not the Claude Code experience, where
  tokens appear as they're produced.

  The capability is half-built: ModelGateway.stream() exists and handles deltas,
  partial-cache safety and barge-in abort — but the DeepAgents path calls
  complete(), and there's no event type to carry a delta even if it did. Closing
  it means wiring HostChatModel to the streaming path plus a model.delta event
  that is emitted but not persisted to the event log (you don't want a row per
  token).

  Two things to watch

  - Tool output streams in full. One tool.completed was 6.4 KB of the 9.4 KB 
    total — the customer list inline, duplicated as text and structuredContent,
    including emails and phone numbers. Every SSE subscriber gets all of it.
    Fine locally; worth a redaction policy before a browser subscribes.
  - model.requested exists in the taxonomy but didn't appear in the stream —
    worth checking whether it's emitted, since it's what a UI would use to show
    "thinking…" at the start of a model call.

  Want me to implement the token-level path, or write this up as a validation
  note in ai-docs/test/?

⏺ Durability is proven. This is the clean result:

  run.created
  run.started     { resumedFromStep: null }
     … worker hard-killed after 13 steps / 6 tool invocations …
     … 56s frozen, no progress, no other worker took it …

  Side effects: exactly 10 invocations — 1 list_customers + 9 insert_row.

  That's the number that matters. Had it restarted from scratch you'd see 15
  (the 5 inserts done before the crash, repeated) and five duplicate rows in
  your sheet. It resumed from step 13 and did only the remaining work.

  What each phase proved:

  ┌────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │        Observation         │            What it establishes             │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Frozen at 13 steps for 56s │ The interruption was real — nothing else   │
  │                            │ silently picked it up                      │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ run.resumed                │ LangGraph checkpoint restored; not a fresh │
  │ {resumedFromStep: 13}      │  start                                     │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 9 inserts, not 14          │ No repeated side effects — the thing v1    │
  │                            │ could never promise                        │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Completed 20s after        │ Recovery is automatic; no operator action  │
  │ restart                    │                                            │
  └────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  This is exactly the gap that motivated the migration. In v1 this run lived in
  RunRegistryService — an in-memory map — so a pod restart lost it entirely, and
  Conductor would have sat waiting for a callback that never came. Here the pod
  died mid-flight and the run finished correctly with no duplicate writes.

  Two honest caveats on what was not tested:

  - I killed between tool calls, not during one. The dangerous case is a crash
    while a non-idempotent tool is in flight — the row may or may not have been
    written, and hpoc can't know. It has assertNoIndeterminateInvocations for
    exactly this: a resumed run finding its own running non-idempotent
    invocation must refuse to retry and dead-letter instead. Worth testing
    separately, since it's the one path where the answer is "stop and ask a
    human."
  - My first attempt was botched — I took down the API and scheduler along with
    the worker, and the kill landed before any real work, so it proved nothing.
    The run above is the valid one.

  Also worth noting: your sheet now has rows from several test runs (3 + 3 + 2 +
  9 + 9), so don't read row count as a correctness signal — tool-invocations is
  the authoritative record.

  Want me to test the crash-during-a-tool-call case next? That's the remaining
  durability claim, and the one with a deliberately uncomfortable answer.

❯ Hell Yeah!!

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The poll never saw running — and that's itself a finding. The invocation row
  is written and settled inside one transaction, so running is never visible
  externally, and a crash rolls it back entirely. Let me trigger the kill from
  ap-executor's log instead, which fires while the call is genuinely in flight.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Exactly the discrepancy predicted: ap-executor processed 3 writes, hpoc 
  recorded only 2. The third transaction rolled back — not even a running row
  survived. Now let me see what resume does with it.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Found a real one — and it's the uncomfortable answer I warned about.

  Crashing during a tool call silently duplicates side effects:

  billa raja        ← written
  Noah Kim          ← written
  billa raja        ← WRITTEN AGAIN after resume
  Noah Kim          ← WRITTEN AGAIN after resume
  Fatima Al-Sayed
  … (7 more)

  11 insert_row invocations for 9 customers. The run reported completed with "I 
  wrote 9 rows to the Google Sheet." No error, no dead-letter, nothing flagged.
  Your sheet has two duplicate rows and every layer believes it succeeded.

  The guard never fired. ToolRuntime.assertNoIndeterminateInvocations exists
  precisely for this — its comment calls it "the most important three lines in 
  this file": a resumed run that finds its own running non-idempotent invocation
  must refuse to retry and say so. It found nothing, because it cannot.

  Why it can't work as written. tool_invocations is inserted as running, the
  tool executes, and settle() marks it — all on the same transaction tx. So:

  - The running state is never visible outside that transaction. My first
    attempt polled the DB 200 times during nine tool calls and saw running
    exactly zero times.
  - On a crash the transaction rolls back, taking the running row with it. The
    evidence at the moment of the kill was unambiguous: ap-executor had
    processed 3 writes, hpoc had recorded 2. The third vanished.

  So the row meant to record "we were mid-effect" is destroyed by the very crash
  it exists to detect. The comment describes the intended design; the
  transaction boundary prevents it.

  The fix is a separate transaction — write the running row and commit it before
  the call, then settle it in another. That's the standard shape for this, and
  it's what makes the existing guard functional rather than decorative. It costs
  one extra round trip per tool call.

  Worth stressing how narrow the window looked and how bad the outcome is: ~1–2
  seconds per tool call, and the result is duplicate financial-grade side
  effects with a completed status. For Relay pieces that send payments, emails
  or refunds rather than spreadsheet rows, that's the failure mode you least
  want to be silent.

  Net across both durability tests:

  ┌───────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │        Crash point        │                    Result                    │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Between steps             │ Resumes from checkpoint, no duplicates —     │
  │                           │ works exactly as advertised                  │
  ├───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ During a non-idempotent   │ Silent duplicate effects, run reports        │
  │ tool call                 │ success, guard never fires                   │
  └───────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Want me to implement the two-transaction fix and re-run this exact test to
  confirm it dead-letters instead?
